## Page 1


HOMOTOPY COHERENT GYSIN FUNCTORIALITY
FR´ED´ERIC D´EGLISE, NIELS FELD, AND FANGZHOU JIN
ABSTRACT. We construct homotopy coherent Gysin pullbacks for arbitrary morphisms between
smooth schemes from the geometry of higher deformation spaces to the normal cone. This ad-
dresses the long-standing problem of organizing Gysin morphisms into a genuinely higher con-
travariant functorial structure. The main input is a higher-dimensional refinement of the Fulton-
MacPherson and Rost constructions: to a flag of closed immersions we attach a higher deformation
space whose ordered boundary produces, by iterated residues and inflation, the higher simplices
of a Gysin functor. We prove the face and degeneracy relations by introducing specialization flags
and confluences, and then rigidify the resulting contractible choices to obtain an honest simplicial
map.
The construction applies to any weak Borel-Moore theory: a stable infinity-categorical co-
efficient system with smooth contravariance, localization, primitive closed pushforwards, A1-
invariance and inflation maps. For such a theory G, and every m ∈Z, we obtain a contravariant
Gysin functor
Glci
m : N(SmS)op −→C
extending smooth pullbacks and closed-immersion Gysin morphisms, and coherent with graph
factorizations in all simplicial degrees.
As a main application, we apply this machinery to Rost-Schmid complexes of MW-homodules
over a noetherian excellent base. We prove that these complexes define motivic objects in the P1-
stable A1-derived category DA1(S, R) representing both cohomological and Borel-Moore Rost-
Schmid theories. In particular, applying the construction to Milnor and Milnor-Witt K-theory
produces Rost-Schmid type extensions of Chow and Chow-Witt groups, equipped with localiza-
tion long exact sequences and homotopy coherent lci pullbacks; on the diagonal these groups
recover the usual Chow and Chow-Witt groups. For the standard examples we also establish
stability under exceptional pullback.

INTRODUCTION
Gysin maps and the coherence problem.
Intersection theory has notably been organized
around the construction of pullback maps for morphisms which are not necessarily flat. Such
maps, sometimes called Gysin maps, are the mechanism by which one turns geometric inter-
sections into functorial operations. In the classical theory two complementary methods play a
central role. The first is the moving-lemma method, going back to Chow: one moves cycles into


## Page 3


HOMOTOPY COHERENT GYSIN FUNCTORIALITY
3
a position in which ordinary flat pullback is available. The second is the deformation-to-the-
normal-cone method, developed and systematized in the Fulton-MacPherson approach and in
Fulton’s intersection theory book [Ful98]. This second construction has the decisive advantage
of working without a base field and in great generality, provided one works with quasi-projective,
or more generally smoothable lci, morphisms.
Rost later recast this circle of ideas in an axiomatic and cycle-theoretic form for cycle modules
[Ros96]. One of the important lessons of Rost’s paper is that the construction of Gysin maps is
not only a problem of defining individual morphisms. Associativity of pullbacks already forces
one to analyze double deformation spaces and to compare several ways of passing through their
boundary strata. Thus the first non-trivial coherence problem for Gysin maps is already visible
in the construction of Chow groups with coefficients.
For motivic homotopy theory this coherence problem becomes unavoidable in all degrees. A
motivic spectrum over a base scheme is built out of Nisnevich sheaves on the smooth site in an
infinity-categorical sense. Therefore a Gysin construction which is meant to produce motivic
objects cannot merely assign a pullback map to each morphism and check associativity up to an
unspecified homotopy. It must describe all higher compositions of Gysin maps coherently. In the
language of quasi-categories [Lur09a], this means that one should construct a simplicial functor
N(SmS)op −→C
with values in a suitable target infinity-category C. Equivalently, one must construct not only
edges, but also higher simplices witnessing all relations among iterated Gysin pullbacks.
In motivic homotopy theory, Gysin functoriality is usually accessed through motivic funda-
mental classes, as in the construction of D´eglise-Jin-Khan [DJK21]. This gives trace and cotrace
morphisms with the expected compatibility in the homotopy category. What is not provided,
however, is a higher coherent compatibility with composition: in general one does not obtain
from these fundamental classes a simplicial, or infinity-categorical, contravariant functor encod-
ing all higher associativity constraints. To our knowledge, the first construction in the literature
which explicitly addresses this higher coherence problem is Levine’s work on the coniveau ho-
motopy tower [Lev06]. Levine’s method is model-categorical and homological, and may be
viewed as a far-reaching refinement of Chow’s moving lemma. Its range is nevertheless con-
strained by the use of moving techniques, in particular by the regularity and low-dimensional
assumptions on the base. By contrast, the Fulton-MacPherson deformation to the normal cone,
although central to intersection theory and to Rost’s treatment of cycle modules, had not previ-
ously been developed into a higher coherent Gysin construction. The main purpose of this paper
is to supply precisely this missing deformation-theoretic approach.
The geometric input which makes such a construction possible is the theory of higher defor-
mation spaces, recently developed by Adrien Dubouloz and Arnaud Mayeux, together with the
complementary language of multi-centered dilatations [May23, DMdS23, DM24]. These spaces
provide the missing higher-dimensional analogue of Rost’s double deformation space. Their
boundary geometry produces the higher Gysin simplices attached to a flag of closed immersions;

FR´ED´ERIC D´EGLISE, NIELS FELD, AND FANGZHOU JIN
after introducing specialization flags and confluences, it also becomes precise enough to control
the simplicial faces and degeneracies up to the A1-homotopies required by the construction.
The geometric idea. Let
σ = (Z0 ,→Z1 ,→· · · ,→Zn)
be a flag of closed immersions between smooth S-schemes. To such a flag one associates a
higher deformation space using the Dubouloz-Mayeux theory
D(σ) −→An
S.
Its coordinate boundary is an ordered strict normal crossings divisor. The open stratum is canon-
ically identified with
Zn × (Gm)n,
while the deepest boundary stratum is a vector bundle over Z0. The basic philosophy of the paper
is that the higher Gysin simplex attached to σ is the iterated residue of this ordered boundary,
followed by the identification of the deepest stratum with Z0 through A1-invariance and by the
inflation maps coming from the Gm-coordinates.
This point of view generalizes simultaneously the ordinary deformation to the normal cone
and Rost’s use of double deformation spaces. In dimension one the boundary gives the usual
specialization map. In dimension two it contains the geometry needed for associativity. In ar-
bitrary dimension it produces an (n −1)-simplex in a mapping space, hence a genuine higher
coherence datum. The role of residues is played by a total boundary construction associated with
the localization cube of the ordered normal crossings divisor.
A key difficulty is that coordinate panels of D(σ) are not literally the deformation spaces of the
simplicial faces dkσ. Instead they are deformation spaces of new flags, called specialization flags,
which remember additional normal-bundle information. These specialization flags are the correct
geometric objects for comparing panels with faces. The comparison with actual faces uses A1-
invariance in an essential way. Dually, degeneracy operators are governed by confluences: maps
on the parameter affine spaces which replace one deformation parameter by a product of two
adjacent parameters. The confluence construction is the geometric source of the homotopies
controlling degeneracies.
Thus the construction is not a formal repackaging of known Gysin maps. It is a new construc-
tion of higher simplices out of the boundary geometry of higher deformation spaces, together
with a proof that these simplices satisfy the face and degeneracy identities up to canonical, con-
tractible choices.
Weak Borel-Moore theories. The deformation construction is designed to work from elemen-
tary input. We do not assume a six-functor formalism. Instead we isolate the exact structure
needed to evaluate localization on higher deformation spaces.
Definition A (Weak Borel-Moore theory, introductory form). Let C be a stable ∞-category. A
weak Borel-Moore theory on SmS, with values in C, is a family of objects
X 7−→Gm(X),
m ∈Z,

Gm(X) are contravariantly functorial for smooth morphisms, in a homotopy coherent sense.
Second, one is given primitive covariant morphisms for trivialized regular closed immersions.
Third, one has localization fiber sequences for principal smooth divisors and for their ordered
normal crossings refinements. Fourth, vector bundle projections are required to act by specified
A1-equivalences. Finally, the theory is equipped with inflation maps

compatible with smooth pullback, localization, residues, and permutations of Gm-coordinates.
The precise list of axioms, including the required specified homotopies, is Definition 2.2.12. In
particular, the primitive closed maps in the axioms go in the covariant direction; the contravariant
Gysin pullbacks for closed immersions are not part of the input, but are produced by the higher
deformation construction.
The main construction theorem. Our first main theorem asserts that this procedure produces
genuine homotopy coherent Gysin functoriality. In the introduction we record the result in the
following streamlined form; the fully precise statement is Theorem 5.7.2.

The proof has two logically distinct parts. First, Sections 2-4 construct the closed-immersion
theory. For a flag σ of closed immersions, the ordered boundary of D(σ) gives a higher Gysin
simplex
Gsimp
m
(σ) ∈MapC
 Gm(Zn), Gm(Z0)

n−1.
The face relations are proved by comparing panels with specialization flags and then using A1-
invariance to pass to the corresponding simplicial faces. The degeneracy relations are proved
by analyzing confluences of deformation parameters. These comparisons are canonical up to
contractible choice.
Second, Section 4 proves a rigidification theorem which turns such contractible-choice data
into an honest simplicial functor. The point is that the construction of higher Gysin simplices
requires controlled choices: inverse data for vector bundle projections, choices of composites,
and fillers for higher spines. The rigidification theorem shows that these choices can be made
coherently once and for all. This is one of the formal cores of the paper.
The passage from closed immersions to arbitrary morphisms between smooth S-schemes is
then obtained in Section 5. Every simplex
X0 −→X1 −→· · · −→Xn
of N(SmS) has a canonical graph flag inside an iterated fiber product. The closed-immersion
simplices associated with this graph flag live on the product vertices, so they do not by them-
selves have the correct faces on the original vertices Xi. Section 5 therefore constructs a pris-
matic comparison whose lower face is the closed graph simplex and whose vertical edges are
the smooth pullbacks from the original vertices to the product vertices. The upper faces of these
prisms are the raw lci simplices. A graph-composition theorem then provides their coherent op-
erator comparisons, and the abstract rigidification theorem turns this simplexwise package into
the contravariant functor on N(SmS)op.
Rost-Schmid complexes and MW-homodules. The main application is to Rost-Schmid com-
plexes of MW-homodules. These objects were introduced in [Fel20, Fel21, DFJ25] as a homo-
logical form of Milnor-Witt coefficients over general bases, designed to unify Chow groups,
Chow-Witt groups, quadratic refinements, and unramified cohomology.
This is part of the
broader program surrounding Ayoub’s conjectural description of the heart of the perverse homo-
topy t-structure on the motivic homotopy category, a t-structure introduced in [Ayo07, BD17].
See [DFJ22].
Let S be noetherian excellent, equipped with a dimension function δ, and let M be an R-linear
MW-homodule over S. The cohomological Rost-Schmid complex
C•(X, Mm)
forms, after the normalization used in Notation 6.1.1, a weak Borel-Moore theory on SmS. The
main theorem therefore gives a homotopy coherent contravariant functoriality for these com-
plexes for all morphisms of smooth S-schemes. This extends Rost’s construction from ordinary
associativity constraints to all higher coherences.

Thus one obtains a functor
H : MW-HomodS −→DA1(S, R),
M 7−→H(M),
as stated in Theorem 6.2.3. This is a representability statement in a linear motivic category and
not merely after forgetting to a more abstract stable homotopy category; it keeps the explicit
Rost-Schmid complexes visible.
The representing object computes both cohomology and Borel-Moore homology. For smooth
schemes, cohomological Rost-Schmid groups are represented by H(M), and for schemes which
are locally closed in smooth schemes the corresponding homological Rost-Schmid groups are
represented by Borel-Moore homology. In particular, the construction is a homological theory
with localization long exact sequences. Off the diagonal degrees it provides a higher Rost-
Schmid theory, close in spirit to higher Chow and Chow-Witt constructions such as those con-
sidered by Bachmann and Yakerson in [BY20], but defined here through Rost-Schmid complexes
and Borel-Moore localization. On the diagonal it recovers the usual Chow and Chow-Witt groups
in the conventions of [DFJ25].
Chow groups, Chow-Witt groups, and purity. The preceding representability theorem be-
comes especially concrete for the MW-homodules associated with Milnor K-theory and Milnor-
Witt K-theory. It gives motivic objects representing Chow groups and Chow-Witt groups, and
it also places these groups inside a higher homological theory with localization long exact se-
quences and homotopy coherent lci pullbacks.

The first two assertions are Theorem 6.3.5; the extraordinary base-change is Proposition 6.3.8.
The same framework also applies to Quillen K′-theory (or Thomason G-theory), and to the
hermitian K-theory when a dualizing complex is available. This is parallel to the role of G-
theory in Borel-Moore motivic homology and weight structures studied by Jin in [Jin16]. Of
course, representability in DA1(S, R) implies representability in motivic stable homotopy cate-
gory SH(S).
Further directions. The construction is deliberately formulated so that it can be used beyond
Rost-Schmid complexes. In particular, it is intended to serve as a geometric engine for future
constructions of higher fundamental classes, and hence of higher trace and cotrace maps, both in
motivic homotopy theory and in ´etale torsion or ℓ-adic settings. The next steps in the surrounding
program are the completion of the attack on the Ayoub conjecture and the application of these
ideas to the niveau filtration on motivic spectra, in connection with the homotopy coniveau meth-
ods of Levine. A central application of the construction developed in this paper is a corrected
formulation and proof of the universal moving lemma of [DFJ23].
Organization of the paper. Section 1 develops the geometry of higher deformation spaces. It
recalls their construction, proves the normal crossings description of their ordered boundary, in-
troduces specialization flags, and studies confluences and the behavior of panels under simplicial
operators.
Section 2 introduces weak Borel-Moore theories and constructs the higher Gysin simplex for a
flag of closed immersions. The construction uses localization cubes for ordered normal crossings
divisors and the inflation package.
Section 3 proves the face and degeneracy relations for these higher Gysin simplices. The proof
is geometric: faces are controlled by specialization flags and A1-invariance, while degeneracies
are controlled by confluences.


## Page 9


HOMOTOPY COHERENT GYSIN FUNCTORIALITY
9
Section 4 proves the abstract rigidification theorem and applies it to obtain a strict simplicial
closed-immersion pullback theory. It also isolates the shared rigidification principle used later in
the gluing arguments.
Section 5 glues the closed-immersion theory with the already available smooth contravari-
ance. The graph factorization of simplices in N(SmS) reduces the construction to the closed-
immersion case plus smooth pullback, and the resulting raw simplices are rigidified to give the
contravariant functor on N(SmS)op.
Section 6 applies the general construction to Rost-Schmid complexes of MW-homodules. It
proves that these complexes form weak Borel-Moore theories, constructs the motivic object
H(M), proves representability of cohomological and Borel-Moore Rost-Schmid groups, spe-
cializes this to Chow and Chow-Witt groups, and proves exceptional base-change and absolute
purity for the standard examples.
Length and verification of the proof. The length of the paper reflects the nature of the construc-
tion. The argument is highly sensitive to variance, shifts, twists, endpoint conventions, and the
ordering of faces and degeneracies. We have therefore chosen to write the technical verifications
explicitly, rather than suppressing them under general coherence statements. This is especially
important in Sections 2-5, where the construction passes from higher deformation spaces to
closed-immersion Gysin simplices, then to simplicial identities, rigidification, and finally to the
prismatic gluing with smooth pullbacks.
Use of AI-assisted tools. During the preparation of this paper, the authors used large language
model tools as interactive assistants for drafting, expanding, and checking parts of the manu-
script. Their use was not autonomous. The mathematical strategy, definitions, statements, and
final proofs are the responsibility of the authors.
More specifically, AI-assisted tools were used in the following ways: to expand human-
provided proof sketches into detailed drafts; to produce referee-style reports on the readability
and internal consistency of the text; to search for possible inconsistencies in notation, variance,
endpoint conventions, shifts, twists, signs, and cross-references; and to help reorganize lengthy
technical arguments. In each case, the output was read, checked, corrected, and, when necessary,
rejected or substantially rewritten by the authors. The workflow typically consisted of a human
mathematical proposal, an AI-assisted expansion or consistency check, human proof-reading and
correction, further AI-assisted auditing, and final human revision. This process was particularly
useful for detecting local inconsistencies in a long coherence-sensitive manuscript, but it did not
replace mathematical judgement or proof.
The authors are aware of the risks attached to such tools, in particular the possibility of pro-
ducing plausible but incorrect mathematical text. For this reason, AI-assisted output was used
only as part of an iterative process of human verification. No AI system is an author of this pa-
per, and the authors take full responsibility for all mathematical claims, proofs, references, and
formulations.
Conventions and notation. Unless stated otherwise, S denotes a qcqs base scheme. All schemes
are implicitly S-schemes, and all fiber products are taken over S unless explicitly indicated. By a


## Page 10


10
FR´ED´ERIC D´EGLISE, NIELS FELD, AND FANGZHOU JIN
vector bundle we mean a locally free O-module of finite rank. In the application to Rost-Schmid
complexes, S is assumed noetherian excellent and equipped with a dimension function; addi-
tional hypotheses such as a pinning or a dualizing complex are stated explicitly when they are
used.
We write ∆for the simplex category and [n] = {0 < · · · < n}. For a simplicial set K,
Kn denotes the set of n-simplices. For an ordinary category D, N(D) denotes its nerve. An ∞-
category is regarded as a simplicial set in the quasi-categorical model. Thus a homotopy coherent
functor from N(D) to an ∞-category C means a map of simplicial sets N(D) →C, equivalently
an object of Fun(N(D), C). Faces and degeneracies are denoted by di and si. By a space we
mean an ∞-groupoid. For an ∞-category C, we write MapC(x, y) for the mapping space. We
use ≃for equivalences in an ∞-category and ∼= for isomorphisms in an ordinary category.

1.2. Multi-deformation spaces and their strata. Let
σ = (Z0 ,→Z1 ,→· · · ,→Zn)

It is degenerate if and only if one of the closed immersions Zk−1 ,→Zk is an identity (equiva-
lently, Zk−1 = Zk for some 1 ≤k ≤n).
The purpose of this subsection is to define the higher deformation space D(σ) and to prove
the geometric properties we will later use (normal crossings boundary, open stratum, deepest
stratum, panelization).

and let

Then, for every 0 ≤k ≤n −1, the pullback of V (tk) ⊂U ×S An

divisor. Equivalently, the image of tk in A is a non-zero-divisor.
Proof. Fix k. Write
B := OU[t0, . . . , tn−1, u0, . . . , un−1],
J := (xi −Tiui)0≤i≤n−1,
so that A = B/J.
We will show that the images of the generators of J in B/(tk) form a regular sequence. This
implies that
tk, (xi −Tiui)0≤i≤n−1
is a regular sequence in B, hence tk is a non-zero-divisor on A.
Modulo tk, the relations xi −Tiui become simply xi for all i ≥k, because Ti is divisible by
tk. By Lemma 1.4.7, the concatenated tuple
(xk, xk+1, . . . , xn−1)
is a regular sequence on U. Hence, after quotienting B/(tk) by these blocks, we obtain a poly-
nomial ring over Uk:
Rk := OUk[t0, . . . , btk, . . . , tn−1, u0, . . . , uk−1, uk, . . . , un−1].
For 0 ≤i ≤k −1, define inductively
Ri := Ri+1/(xi −Tiui),
where xi denotes the pullback of the block xi to Uk. We claim, by descending induction on i,
that Ti is a non-zero-divisor on Ri.
For i = k −1, the element Tk−1 = t0 · · · tk−1 is a non-zero-divisor on the polynomial ring Rk.
Modulo Tk−1, the block xk−1 −Tk−1uk−1 reduces to xk−1, and xk−1 is a regular sequence on
Uk because Uk−1 ,→Uk is a regular immersion. Therefore Tk−1 remains a non-zero-divisor after
quotienting by xk−1 −Tk−1uk−1, i.e. on Rk−1.
Now assume 0 ≤i < k −1 and that Ti+1 is a non-zero-divisor on Ri+1. Since
Ti+1 = Ti ti+1,
it follows that Ti is also a non-zero-divisor on Ri+1. Hence, componentwise, the block xi −Tiui
is a regular sequence on Ri+1, because each equation is linear in a fresh variable with non-zero-
divisor coefficient Ti.
Moreover, modulo Ti, every previously imposed relation xj −Tjuj with j > i reduces to xj,
since each Tj is divisible by Ti. Thus
Ri+1/TiRi+1
is a polynomial algebra over OUi+1. In that quotient, the block xi is a regular sequence because
Ui ,→Ui+1 is a regular immersion and the images of xi form a basis of its conormal sheaf.
Therefore Ti remains a non-zero-divisor after quotienting by xi −Tiui, i.e. on Ri.

This proves the induction. In particular, the image of J in B/(tk) is generated by a regular
sequence. Consequently tk is a non-zero-divisor on A = B/J, as required.

Remark 1.4.17 (Intrinsic form of Πdeep). Lemma 1.4.14 gives a stronger description than Lemma 1.4.16(2).
Under the canonical direct-sum identifications of the deepest strata, the morphism
Πdeep : H[n−2](Spk(σ)) −→H[n−2](dkσ)
is the identity on the summands corresponding to indices different from k −1, k, and on the
skipped stage it is the composite
NZk−1/Zk|Z0 ⊕Nk,k+1|Z0 −→NZk−1/Zk|Z0 ,→NZk−1/Zk+1|Z0.
In particular, Πdeep is fiberwise linear over π0 : Nk,k+1|Z0 →Z0.
Remark 1.4.18 (Why the global comparison morphism Π is the right one). The morphism
Π : D(Spk(σ)) −→D(dkσ)
is the canonical comparison map between the two global deformation spaces attached to panel
restriction and simplicial face thanks to the universal property of the deformation space (see
[DM24, Prop. 2.4]).
On the open stratum, Π is simply
(ik,n ◦p) × id
by Lemma 1.4.16(4). On the deepest stratum, its behavior is governed by Lemma 1.4.14.
One could instead consider the base-changed morphism
D(Spk(σ)) −→D(dkσ) ×Zn Zk,
but this auxiliary map is generally poorly behaved: its fiber dimension typically jumps between
the generic and special fibers, so it is not the natural object for the later intersection-theoretic
constructions. The point of Π is precisely that it is the canonical global morphism compatible
with the simplicial geometry of the deformation spaces.

2. HIGHER GYSIN SIMPLICES FOR CLOSED IMMERSIONS
2.1. Simplices, mapping spaces, and whiskering. We work over the fixed base scheme S and
we consider a pointed ∞-category C admitting finite limits. We work with the Joyal model:
thus C is a quasicategory (i.e. a simplicial set satisfying the inner horn-filling condition), and an
∞-functor F : C →D means a morphism of simplicial sets.
2.1.1. Let us describe the global strategy following in Sections 2-4. We will work with a weak
Borel-Moore theory (see Definition 2.2.12), which to a smooth S-scheme X and an integer n ∈
Z, associates an object Gm(X) ∈C. The aim of the whole section is to build a higher coherent
contravariant functoriality out of this data associated with closed-immersions. We proceed in
three stages.
In the current section, we associate to every
σ ∈N(Smimm
S
)n
the higher Gysin simplex:
Gsimp

 Gm(Zn), Gm(Z0)

n−1.
In Section 3, we prove that these simplices satisfy the required face and degeneracy identities,
canonically up to contractible choice. In Section 4, we rigidify those contractible choices and
obtain a strict simplicial map
eGm : N(Smimm
S
)op →C.
Notations 2.1.2. Let
sSet := Fun(∆op, Set).
For n ≥0, the boundary of ∆n is the subobject
∂∆n :=

## Page 60

FR´ED´ERIC D´EGLISE, NIELS FELD, AND FANGZHOU JIN
For vertices (objects) x, y ∈C0, we write MapC(x, y) for the mapping space, defined as the
usual Kan complex associated to C. In particular, MapC(x, y) is a Kan complex and its 0-
simplices are the edges x →y in C. (See [Lur09b, §1.2.2].) Precisely, we take MapC(x, y)
to be the right mapping space MapR
C (x, y) of [Lur09b, §1.2.2], so that pre- and post-composition
induce morphisms of simplicial sets functorially in both variables.
Convention 2.1.3 (Structural data vs. contractible-choice data). We systematically distinguish
two kinds of simplices.
(1) Structural simplices. By structural data we mean simplices that are part of the chosen input.
In this paper, structural simplices include:
• the functoriality homotopies and higher coherences built into the weak Borel-Moore the-
ories,
• the chosen inverse data for vector bundle projections,
• the localization fiber sequences and their compatibility data,
• and any simplices obtained from these by simplicial functoriality, whiskering, or compo-
sition.
Thus, structural simplices are part of the formal package and are regarded as fixed once the theory
is fixed.
(2) Homotopies and higher coherences in mapping spaces. A homotopy between parallel
morphisms f, g : x →y in C means a 1-simplex in the Kan complex MapC(x, y) with endpoints f
and g. More generally, an n-simplex in MapC(x, y) encodes an n-parameter family of morphisms
and higher coherences among lower-dimensional data.
(3) Contractible-choice data (“canonical up to contractible choice”). Sometimes we use the
fact that a certain extension or filler space is contractible. Formally, if i : A ,→B is a trivial
cofibration of simplicial sets and K is a Kan complex, then restriction induces a trivial Kan
fibration
MapsSet(B, K) −→MapsSet(A, K),
so the space of fillers of a given boundary datum is contractible. Whenever we appeal to such a
fact, we may choose a filler once and for all, and any later construction depending on this choice
is understood only up to the resulting contractible indeterminacy.
(4) Terminology. We say structural when the simplex is part of the chosen input data, and
canonical up to contractible choice when it is obtained only from a contractible filler space as in
(3).
Definition 2.1.4 (Simplices with fixed endpoints). Let x, y ∈C0. For n ≥0, an n-simplex from
x to y (or n-arrow) means an element of MapC(x, y)n, i.e. a simplicial map ∆n →MapC(x, y).
Thus:
• 0-simplices are morphisms x →y,
• 1-simplices are homotopies between morphisms,
• higher simplices encode higher coherences.

Definition 2.1.5 (Whiskering). Let a : x′ →x and b : y →y′ be morphisms in C. Functoriality
of mapping spaces in each variable induces simplicial maps
(−) ◦a : MapC(x, y) −→MapC(x′, y),
b ◦(−) : MapC(x, y) −→MapC(x, y′).
Their composite
b ◦(−) ◦a : MapC(x, y) −→MapC(x′, y′)
is called whiskering (by a on the right and by b on the left).
Lemma 2.1.6 (Whiskering is simplicial). For fixed a and b, the whiskering map of Defini-
tion 2.1.5 is a morphism of simplicial sets. Equivalently, it commutes with all face and de-
generacy operators.
Proof. In the quasicategory model, (x, y) 7→MapC(x, y) is functorial in both variables, and the
induced pre- and post-composition maps on mapping spaces are simplicial. Composing these
simplicial maps yields the whiskering map, hence it is simplicial.
□

Lemma 2.1.11. In the situation of Definition 2.1.10, the projection

Proof. Since C≃is a Kan complex, the outer horn inclusions Λ2

Lemma 2.1.12 (Contractibility of inverse-equivalence data). For any equivalence f in a quasi-
category C, the ∞-groupoid of inverse data for f is contractible.
Proof. Definition 2.1.10 gives an explicit simplicial model Inv(f) for the space of inverse data
of f, and Lemma 2.1.11 shows that Inv(f) is a contractible Kan complex.
□
Remark 2.1.13 (Practical use of Lemma 2.1.12). In the sequel, Lemma 2.1.12 is used only to
justify that whenever a morphism in C is known to be an equivalence, one may choose an inverse
morphism together with unit/counit homotopies, and that all such choices form a contractible
space.

Remark 2.2.13 (Input closed pushforwards versus output closed pullbacks). The maps
i∗: Gm−c(Z) →Gm(X)
for trivialized regular immersions are part of the axiomatic input. By contrast, the higher Gysin
pullbacks
i∗: Gm(X) →Gm(Z)
for arbitrary closed immersions are constructed later from deformation spaces, localization, A1-
invariance, and the inflation package. Thus the notation i∗belongs to the input formalism,
whereas i∗belongs to the output of the higher Gysin construction.
Notations 2.2.14 (inflation package). We refer collectively to Axioms (wBM10)-(wBM14) as
the inflation package.
Remark 2.2.15. Examples of weak Borel-Moore theories will be given in Section 6; see in par-
ticular Proposition 6.1.4.
2.3. Localization cubes and the coherent total boundary simplex. In this subsection, we fix
a weak Borel-Moore theory G in the sense of Definition 2.2.12.

KX,H•(m) : P([n −1])op −→C

quences, so the displayed square commutes up to the canonical homotopy.

Remark 2.4.7 (Why face-compatibility is non-formal). Although σ 7→D(σ) is compatible with
degeneracies via parameter confluences (Lemma 1.5.1), restricting D(σ) to a coordinate panel
{tk = 0} does not recover the simplicial face dkσ of the nerve in general. Rather, by Lemma 1.3.7
it produces the deformation space of the specialized flag Spk(σ), which involves normal bundles.
Consequently, the simplicial identities for the degree-m higher Gysin construction require
combining: (i) these geometric comparison isomorphisms, and (ii) A1-invariance / vector bundle
equivalences in C to transport along the normal-bundle replacements.

3. SIMPLICIAL IDENTITIES FOR HIGHER GYSIN SIMPLICES
The goal of this section is to verify the simplicial identities satisfied by the higher Gysin
simplices constructed in Section 2.

patibilities are canonical only up to contractible choice; the rigidification of those choices is
postponed to Section 4.

There are three regimes:

and the extra boundary step cancels by inert Gm-cancellation.

The proofs separate into: (i) a fiber-category lemma comparing faces of ∂simp with panel
restriction of cubes, (ii) geometric identifications of the panel faces, and (iii) collapse/Koszul
bookkeeping for the precomposition map βσ,m.

of the structural data of the weak Borel-Moore theory.

Lemma 3.2.17 (Tor-independence for base change of sections of smooth morphisms). Let π :
P →X be a smooth morphism and let s : X ,→P be a section (hence a regular immersion).
For any morphism g : X′ →X, form the cartesian square
X′
P ′ := P ×X X′
X
P.
s′
g
s
Then the square is Tor-independent.
Proof. Tor-independence is local on P for the ´etale topology, so it is enough to check the claim
´etale-locally in a neighbourhood of the image s(X) ⊂P.
Since π : P →X is smooth and s is a section, ´etale-locally on P around every point of s(X)
the pair (P, s) is isomorphic over X to the projection with its zero section:
(X × Ad →X, X ,→X × Ad).
After base change along g : X′ →X, the same is true for
(P ′, s′) = (P ×X X′, X′ ,→P ×X X′).
Thus ´etale-locally the cartesian square of the statement becomes
X′
X′ × Ad
X
X × Ad,
where the horizontal arrows are zero sections. The zero section is cut out by the regular sequence
x1, . . . , xd,
so its Koszul complex resolves the structure sheaf of the section and remains exact after arbitrary
base change. Hence all higher Tor groups vanish.
Since this vanishing is ´etale-local, the original square is Tor-independent.
□

3.4. Degeneracy compatibility.
3.4.1. Strategy and geometry on parameters.
3.4.2 (Strategy for degeneracies). Geometrically, the degeneracy skσ repeats one immersion.
On deformation parameters this is implemented by the panelization map µk, which duplicates
one coordinate hyperplane and hence inserts one extra boundary component in the ordered SNC
divisor on the deformation space.
For k = n, this appears directly as an appended inert last divisor, so the localization tower
gains one extra terminal boundary step
δlast
t
: Gm+n+1(U × Gm)[−n −1] −→Gm+n(U)[−n].

last position via the permutation formalism of §3.1. In both cases, after precomposition by the
relevant collapse map, the extra inert step cancels by Axiom (wBM12), and the result is exactly
the corresponding simplicial degeneracy.

4. RIGIDIFICATION OF SIMPLICIAL IDENTITIES
4.1. Simplex-coordinate preliminaries.

Convention 4.1.2 (Opposite-simplex indexing). Let

be a geometric flag. We write σop for the corresponding simplex of N(Smimm

)op. Under the

corresponds to the geometric vertex Zn−a.
Consequently, for cofaces and codegeneracies one has
σop ◦δa = (dn−aσ)op,

Equivalently, when statements are written in terms of the geometric flag σ, the geometric opera-
tors di and sj correspond respectively to the simplex-coordinate operators δn−i and ςn−j.
Remark 4.1.3. Throughout the remainder of this subsection, the integer m ∈Z fixed in Nota-
tion 4.1.1 remains fixed. We rigidify the degree-m assignment
σ 7−→Gm(σ).
Accordingly, all coherent choice spaces, matching objects, evaluation maps, and rigidifications
carry an implicit dependence on m.
4.1.4 (Plan of §4). We proceed in six steps.
(1) Translate face and degeneracy compatibilities into simplex coordinates (Corollaries 4.1.6-
4.1.9).
(2) Define raw choice spaces Choicesm(σ) and the constant raw evaluation map (Defini-
tion 4.2.1, Lemma 4.2.4).
(3) Build operator-comparison complexes and transported comparison data (Lemmas 4.2.8-
4.2.9).
(4) Refine the raw choice spaces to coherent choice spaces by means of finite skeletal sec-
tions and an inverse-limit construction (Lemma 4.2.18, Proposition 4.2.20).
(5) Prove that the global projection
pm : Ecoh
m
→N(Smimm
S
)op
has the required boundary-lifting property, and use this to choose a global coherent sec-
tion extending the prescribed 1-skeleton (Proposition 4.2.23, Proposition 4.2.24).
(6) Extract coherent operator comparisons from the global section and use them to construct
the strict rigidification of the closed-immersion pullback package by skeletal induction
(Lemma 4.3.2, Theorem 4.4.8, Theorem 4.5.5).
Remark 4.1.5 (Relation to the literature). The rigidification result proved in this subsection is
formally quite general. We do not know a reference in the literature that states exactly Theo-
rem 4.4.8 in the form needed here, so the argument is included for completeness.
The general philosophy is however classical: one replaces homotopy-coherent simplexwise
data by a strict simplicial map after organizing the relevant comparison choices into contractible
spaces and proving suitable extension or lifting statements. Our construction is close in spirit to
several well-known rigidification and rectification results, notably:


## Page 134


134
FR´ED´ERIC D´EGLISE, NIELS FELD, AND FANGZHOU JIN
• Cordier-Porter on categories of homotopy coherent diagrams [CP86],
• Dugger-Spivak on rigidification of quasi-categories [DS11],
• the homotopy coherent nerve viewpoint, especially the cubical boundary-extension de-
scription in Kerodon, Corollary 2.4.6.13, together with Riehl’s exposition [Lur24, Rie23],
• Riehl-Verity on homotopically unique extensions, where the relevant spaces of extensions
are contractible Kan complexes [RV16],
• and the rectification statement in Kerodon, Corollary 5.6.5.18 and Remark 5.6.5.19 [Lur24].
Thus the subsection should be viewed as a self-contained rigidification argument adapted to
the specific coherent choice spaces arising from the higher closed-immersion pullback simplices.

∂∆n ,→∆n

X be the subobject generated by simplices of degree ≤n under degeneracies; then X =

along ∂∆n. The relative-skeleton construction for a mono A ,→B gives the claimed generation
up to retract.

4.2.10 (Formal packaging of coherent choices). The next construction packages vertexwise co-
herent choices. Given simplicial choice spaces with strictly functorial pullback maps, it produces
a simplicial set whose simplices over σ : ∆n →K record coherent families of vertices in the
local choice spaces attached to the faces of σ. We use this only as a bookkeeping device for strict
compatibility. It should not be confused with a Grothendieck construction whose fibers recover
the full local simplicial sets.

and degeneracies of Eσ,N are built into the construction, rather than being imposed simplexwise
afterwards.

Lemma 4.4.3 (Restriction to the boundary is a categorical fibration). Let C be a quasicategory
and n ≥1. Then restriction along ∂∆n ,→∆n induces a categorical fibration
ρn : Fun(∆n, C) −→Fun(∂∆n, C).
In particular, the induced map on maximal Kan complexes
ρ≃
n : Fun(∆n, C)≃−→Fun(∂∆n, C)≃
is a Kan fibration of simplicial sets.
Proof. This is [Lur24, 01F3 and 01F4].
□
Lemma 4.4.4 (Skeletal attachment by nondegenerate simplices). Let L be a simplicial set and
let n ≥1. Write Lnd

square
a

n

skn(L).
Proof. This is the standard description of the skeletal filtration of a simplicial set: skn(L) is
obtained from skn−1(L) by attaching one copy of ∆n for each nondegenerate n-simplex, along
its boundary.
□
Lemma 4.4.5 (Contractible space of composites in a Kan complex). Let X be a Kan complex,
and let
x
u−→y
v−→z
be composable edges in X. Then the simplicial set of 2-simplices
θ ∈X2
with
d2(θ) = u,
d0(θ) = v
is a contractible Kan complex.
Proof. This is folklore (see [Lur24, 01PL]) but here’s a short proof. The simplicial set of the
statement is the fiber of the restriction map
MapsSet(∆2, X) −→MapsSet(Λ2
1, X)
over the horn determined by u and v. Since Λ2
1 ,→∆2 is inner anodyne and X is Kan, this
restriction map is a trivial Kan fibration. Hence the fiber is a contractible Kan complex.
□

deduce from a weak Borel-Moore theory G a fully contravariant functor on SmS which extends
the case of closed immersions treated in Theorem 4.5.5. Let us roughly explain our procedure.

cancelled inside the graph simplex; they are recorded as vertical data and cancelled only through
the section-cancelled base-change lemma below.

Lemma 5.2.3 (Basic properties of the canonical graph flag). Let τ ∈N(SmS)n. Then:
(1) each γτ,r is a closed immersion in SmS;
(2) each γτ,r is a regular immersion;
(3) qτ : Pn(τ) →Xn is smooth.
Proof. For each r, the morphism γτ,r is the graph over S of the morphism
fr ◦qτ,r−1 : Pr−1(τ) →Xr.
Since Xr →S is separated, this graph is a closed immersion. Moreover
Pr(τ) = Pr−1(τ) ×S Xr →Pr−1(τ)
is smooth, and γτ,r is a section of this smooth morphism. Hence γτ,r is a regular immersion.
If n = 0, then qτ = idX0. If n ≥1, then
Pn(τ) = Pn−1(τ) ×S Xn
and qτ is the second projection. Since Pn−1(τ) →S is smooth as a finite product of smooth
S-schemes, qτ is its base change along Xn →S. Therefore qτ is smooth.
□

phisms, this pushout-product is again inner anodyne.

Lemma 5.5.14 (Deformation space of a section over the base). Let
p : P →X
be a smooth morphism in SmS, and let
s : X ,→P
be a section. Write
D(s) →A1
S
for the deformation space of the regular immersion s, let

be its special fiber, and let

be its open stratum.
Then the morphism
p × idA1 : P × A1 →X × A1
induces a canonical morphism
ep : D(s) −→X × A1
which is smooth. It restricts to:
(1) the vector bundle projection
π : N = NX/P →X
on the special fiber over 0,
(2) and
p × idGm : P × Gm →X × Gm
on the open complement Us ≃P × Gm.

Proof. The morphism ep is induced functorially from p × idA1 by the construction of deforma-
tion to the normal cone. On the open complement A1 \ {0} = Gm, the deformation space is
canonically
Us ≃P × Gm,
and ep is exactly p × idGm. On the special fiber, the induced morphism is the normal bundle
projection
π : NX/P →X.
It remains to show that ep is smooth. This is local on source and target for the ´etale topology.
Around every point of s(X) ⊂P, the pair (P, s) is ´etale-locally over X isomorphic to the zero
section of an affine bundle
X ,→X × Ad.
For the zero section, the deformation space is canonically
D(s) ≃X × Ad × A1,
which is smooth over X × A1. Thus ep is smooth ´etale-locally near the special fiber, and it is
already smooth on the open part Us. Hence ep is smooth everywhere.
□

Remark 5.5.18 (Why the top face is not formal). The side data of a graph prism do not formally
determine the upper face. Indeed, the vertical morphisms
q∗
τ,r : Gm(Xr) →Gm(Pr(τ))
are smooth pullbacks, but they are not equivalences in general. Thus one cannot cancel them
from the side squares. Already for n = 2, a bottom triangle together with the three side squares
does not force a top triangle in an arbitrary quasicategory. Consequently the construction of
Mprism
m
(τ) requires a genuine graph-composition argument, not only an inner-anodyne prism-
filling argument.

to an equivalence in Fun(∆1, C)≃.

6. APPLICATION: REPRESENTABILITY OF ROST-SCHMID COMPLEXES
6.1. Rost-Schmid complexes of MW-homodules.
Notations 6.1.1. We record the main cycle-theoretic source of examples for the weak Borel-
Moore formalism of Definition 2.2.12. Throughout this subsection, let S be a noetherian ex-
cellent scheme equipped with a dimension function δ, and let M be an R-linear homological
Milnor-Witt cycle module over S, or MW-homodule, in the sense of [DFJ25, Def. 1.2.2]. We
write D(R) for the derived ∞-category of R-modules.
Let X be an essentially lci S-scheme. We use the cohomological Rost-Schmid convention of
[DFJ25, 1.3.7]: for a line bundle L on X, the cocycle complex is
Cp(X, Mq, L) =
M
x∈X(p)
Mq−p
 κ(x), ωX/S,x ⊗L∨
x

,
with differential induced by the residues of M. We abbreviate
C•(X, Mq) := C•(X, Mq, OX).
Thus, if X/S is smooth of relative dimension d, this convention is related to the homological
Rost-Schmid complex by
Cp(X, Mq, L) = Cd−p
 X, Mq−d, ωX/S ⊗L∨
.
The shift in the following definition is part of the normalization. It is the normalization for which
smooth pullbacks have degree zero, closed immersions of codimension c give maps m −c →
m without any extra suspension, and the Gm-contractions have precisely the shift required in
Definition 2.2.12.
Definition 6.1.2 (The Rost-Schmid weak Borel-Moore object). For X ∈SmS and m ∈Z, set
GRS
m (X) := C•(X, Mm)[m]
∈
D(R).
When no confusion is possible, we write simply Gm(X) for GRS

Remark 6.1.3 (Why the shift). With cohomological conventions, an essentially smooth morphism
p : Y →X induces a pullback
p! : C•(X, Mm) −→C•(Y, Mm)
of degree zero. A regular closed immersion i : Z ,→X of codimension c, together with a
trivialization of det NZ/X, induces a cohomological proper pushforward
C•(Z, Mm−c) −→C•(X, Mm)[c].
After shifting the source by [m −c] and the target by [m], this becomes a morphism
Gm−c(Z) −→Gm(X).
Likewise, multiplication by n ordered units raises the Milnor-Witt degree from m to m + n, and
the identity
Gm+n(X × Gn
m)[−n] = C•(X × Gn
m, Mm+n)[m]
explains the shift appearing in the contraction maps.
Proposition 6.1.4 (Rost-Schmid complexes give a weak Borel-Moore theory). Consider the set-
ting of the preceding definition. Then the assignment
X 7−→Gm(X) = C•(X, Mm)[m]
for X ∈SmS and m ∈Z is a weak Borel-Moore theory on SmS with values in D(R), in the
sense of Definition 2.2.12.
Proof. We verify the fourteen pieces of Definition 2.2.12. The basic maps on Rost-Schmid com-
plexes are those of [DFJ25, §1.4]: proper pushforward, essentially smooth pullback, multiplica-
tion by units, multiplication by η, and boundary maps. The facts used below are the compati-
bilities of [DFJ25, §1.5], the localization sequence [DFJ25, 1.5.8], and homotopy invariance for
vector bundles [DFJ25, Thm. 2.1.4]. Equalities of maps of complexes give constant specified ho-
motopies in D(R); whenever an inverse to a quasi-isomorphism is needed, we choose coherent
inverse data by Definition 2.1.10 and Lemma 2.1.11.
Axiom (wBM1): assignment. This is Definition 6.1.2.
Axiom (wBM2): smooth contravariance. Let p : Y →X be a smooth morphism in SmS. The
essentially smooth pullback of [DFJ25, 1.4.2], in cohomological conventions, gives a morphism
of complexes
p! : C•(X, Mm) −→C•(Y, Mm).
After the common shift [m], we denote the resulting morphism by
p∗: Gm(X) −→Gm(Y ).
For composable smooth morphisms Z
q−→Y
p−→X, [DFJ25, Prop. 1.5.1(2)] gives the equality
(p ◦q)! = q! ◦p!. Together with the evident identity statement, these equalities define a strict,
hence homotopy coherent, simplicial map
Gsm
m : N(Smsm
S )op −→D(R).

It remains to recall why the levels are motivic. Nisnevich descent follows from the pointwise
decomposition of the Rost-Schmid complex for a Nisnevich square, together with the smooth
base-change compatibility of [DFJ25, Prop. 1.5.1(3)]. A1-invariance follows from homotopy
invariance for vector bundles, [DFJ25, Thm. 2.1.4]. Therefore H(M) is an ΩGm-spectrum in
DA1(S, R).
Finally let α : M →N be a morphism of MW-homodules. For every S-field E and every in-
teger n, the map αE,n : Mn(E) →Nn(E) commutes with the structure maps (D1′), . . . , (D4′).
Hence it induces morphisms of Rost-Schmid complexes
α# : C•(X, Mm, L) −→C•(X, Nm, L)
for all X, m, L. These maps commute with smooth pullbacks, proper pushforwards, multiplica-
tion by units, η, residues, localization, and the contraction maps. They therefore assemble into a
map of Gm-spectra H(α) : H(M) →H(N), functorially in α.

The point of the present paper is that these groups are not merely defined by explicit Rost-
Schmid complexes. They are represented by motivic objects constructed from the higher coher-
ent Gysin formalism. Thus they come with cohomology on essentially lci schemes, Borel-Moore
homology on singular schemes, localization, homotopy invariance, Thom isomorphisms, and ho-
motopy coherent lci pullbacks. The remaining structure not constructed here is the multiplicative
one: the cup-product and intersection-product refinements should come from a future enhance-
ment of H(M) to a commutative algebra object.
